TI  - A Podcast on Osimertinib After CRT in Unresectable Stage III EGFR-Mutated 
      Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (NSCLC): Current and Future Perspectives.
LID - 10.1007/s11523-026-01250-z [doi]
AB  - Epidermal growth factor receptor-mutated (EGFRm) non-small cell lung cancer 
      (NSCLC) disease biology may differ from EGFR-wild type NSCLC, with an increased 
      risk of distant metastases in unresectable stage III EGFRm NSCLC. Osimertinib is 
      an established first treatment for patients with EGFRm advanced NSCLC, 
      effectively treating EGFRm tumours. The phase III LAURA study, which assessed the 
      efficacy and safety of osimertinib for patients with locally advanced, 
      unresectable stage III EGFRm NSCLC who completed chemoradiotherapy (CRT) without 
      disease progression, demonstrated a statistically significant improvement with 
      osimertinib in progression-free survival (PFS), with a median PFS of 39.1 months, 
      versus 5.6 months with placebo. The safety profile was as expected based on the 
      known profiles of osimertinib and CRT. On the basis of the LAURA results, 
      osimertinib has now been approved for the treatment of unresectable stage III 
      EGFRm NSCLC. Given the implementation of the LAURA regimen in multiple countries, 
      this podcast highlights the potential challenges that must be considered by 
      healthcare professionals when administering osimertinib in clinical practice, and 
      how these may be mitigated. Considerations of the impact of the 
      'treat-to-progression' approach, a paradigm shift for the treatment of stage III 
      unresectable NSCLC, and patient-centred care, including the importance of shared 
      decision-making, will also be discussed. Overall, continuous systemic treatment 
      post-CRT may be key to improving long-term outcomes and represents a mindset 
      shift for both healthcare professionals and patients. Therefore, we may need to 
      consider unresectable stage III EGFRm NSCLC as a chronic disease, requiring 
      long-term treatment to maintain suppression of disease.The podcast and transcript 
      can be viewed below the abstract of the online version of the manuscript. 
      Alternatively, the podcast can be downloaded here:  https://doi.org/ 
      https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.33191613 . Supplementary file1 (MP4 306769 
      KB).

TI  - Jideytro (zidesamtinib) for previously treated ros1-positive non-small cell lung 
      cancer: a new era in post-tki therapy.
PG  - 6229-6231
LID - 10.1097/MS9.0000000000005662 [doi]
AB  - ROS1-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is a distinct oncogene-driven 
      subtype that commonly affects younger, never-smokers and frequently develops 
      central nervous system metastases and kinase-domain resistance mutations. 
      Although first- and second-generation ROS1 tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs) have 
      improved outcomes, most patients eventually progress after treatment. On 22 July 
      2026, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved Jideytro (zidesamtinib) for 
      adults with locally advanced or metastatic ROS1-positive NSCLC previously treated 
      with a ROS1 TKI. Zidesamtinib is a selective, brain-penetrant, macrocyclic ROS1 
      inhibitor that spares tropomyosin receptor kinase (TRK) signaling while 
      maintaining activity against diverse resistance mutations, including G2032R. In 
      the pivotal ARROS-1 trial, it produced durable responses in heavily pretreated 
      patients, including those with brain metastases, with low treatment 
      discontinuation. Although prospective comparisons with crizotinib, entrectinib, 
      repotrectinib, and taletrectinib are needed, zidesamtinib represents an important 
      addition to post-TKI therapy for ROS1-positive NSCLC.

TI  - Central nervous system efficacy of third generation vs. first generation 
      EGFR-TKIs in the first line treatment for EGFR-mutated non-small cell lung cancer 
      with central nervous system metastases: a systematic review and meta-analysis of 
      phase 3 randomized controlled trials.
PG  - 1009
LID - 10.21037/jtd-2026-1571 [doi]
LID - 1009
AB  - BACKGROUND: Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR)-mutant non-small cell lung 
      cancer (NSCLC) with central nervous system (CNS) metastases poses significant 
      treatment challenges, as first-generation EGFR-tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs) 
      (FGET) show limited CNS efficacy. Although third-generation EGFR-TKIs (TGET) 
      exhibit superior CNS penetration, the optimal choice for first-line treatment 
      remains debated. This meta-analysis aims to resolve this controversy by directly 
      comparing the CNS efficacy of TGET vs. FGET in patients with EGFR-mutant NSCLC 
      and CNS metastases. METHODS: A thorough literature search was performed for phase 
      3 randomized controlled trials (RCTs) comparing TGET and FGET in patients with 
      EGFR-mutated NSCLC and CNS metastases. The primary outcome was 
      CNS-progression-free survival (PFS), and secondary outcomes included overall 
      survival (OS), PFS, CNS response rates, and adverse events (AEs). The study was 
      registered in PROSPERO (ID: CRD42024629581). RESULTS: A total of 5 phase 3 RCTs 
      (AENEAS, FLAURA, FURLONG, IBIO-103, and LASER301) met the inclusion criteria. 
      TGET demonstrated superior efficacy in CNS-PFS [hazard ratio (HR): 0.44 (0.33, 
      0.58), P<0.001], PFS [HR: 0.44 (0.35, 0.54), P<0.001], CNS-objective response 
      rate (ORR) [risk ratio (RR): 1.32 (1.15, 1.52), P<0.001], CNS-complete response 
      (CR) [RR: 3.40 (1.39, 8.28), P=0.007], and CNS-duration of response (DOR) [HR: 
      0.39 (0.21, 0.73), P=0.003]. The CNS-PFS rate at 4-24 months and PFS at 10-24 
      months were significantly higher in the TGET group. OS data were available from 
      only 2 of the 5 included phase 3 RCTs. In this analysis, OS numerically favored 
      the TGET group but did not reach statistical significance [HR: 0.79 (0.55, 1.13), 
      P=0.20]. At the cutoff time, more CNS progressions were found in the FGET [RR: 
      0.59 (0.44, 0.80), P=0.001]. Additionally, less grade 3-5 treatment-related AEs 
      (TRAEs) were found in the TGET group [RR: 0.67 (0.46, 0.98), P=0.04]. 
      CONCLUSIONS: TGET appeared to be better than FGET for patients with EGFR-mutated 
      NSCLC and CNS metastases, showing improved survival, CNS efficacy, and safety.
CI  - (c) AME Publishing Company.

TI  - Tumor-associated macrophages beyond M1/M2 in NSCLC: spatially organized programs, 
      therapy resistance, and translational targeting.
PG  - 1960553
LID - 10.3389/fonc.2026.1960553 [doi]
LID - 1960553
AB  - Tumor-associated macrophages (TAMs) are abundant and functionally diverse 
      components of the lung tumor microenvironment (TME) and are associated with 
      therapeutic response and resistance in non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC). 
      Durable responses to immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs) and targeted therapies 
      remain limited, with TAM-associated immune evasion and therapy-refractory 
      microenvironments representing potentially relevant components of this resistance 
      landscape. This narrative review prioritizes evidence from human NSCLC specimens 
      and clinically annotated cohorts, especially from single-cell RNA sequencing 
      (scRNA-seq) and spatial transcriptomics, while integrating relevant mechanistic 
      evidence from NSCLC experimental systems, animal models, and related cancer 
      contexts. Moving beyond the M1/M2 paradigm, we synthesize convergent findings to 
      describe recurring, niche-resolved TAM programs. Across NSCLC, we highlight 
      recurrent SPP1-associated, TREM2-associated, and C1q/LAM-like macrophage programs 
      as a practical framework for integrating recurrent TAM states across spatial and 
      treatment contexts. We propose an integrative framework in which TAM state 
      dynamics are associated with, and may contribute to, therapy-refractory 
      phenotypes during (i) ICI therapy through immune exclusion, (ii) 
      oncogene-targeted therapy through macrophage-associated bypass signaling and 
      survival plasticity, particularly in EGFR-TKI resistance, and (iii) 
      post-radiochemotherapy microenvironmental remodeling, with potential implications 
      for residual disease ecosystems that require further clinical validation, 
      particularly with respect to recurrence and relapse. TAM program signatures and 
      spatial organization provide a rationale for investigating biomarker-guided 
      stratification and macrophage-directed combination strategies targeting 
      state-niche axes. Future work should prioritize clinically validated, state- and 
      niche-resolved multimodal biomarkers and prospective translational studies to 
      support rational pairing with ICIs and targeted therapies.

TI  - Green Spectrofluorimetric Strategies for Capmatinib and Curcumin Determination.
PG  - e70645
LID - 10.1002/bio.70645 [doi]
AB  - The growing convergence between precision oncology and natural-product-based 
      therapeutics has fostered innovative strategies that integrate molecularly 
      targeted anticancer agents with biologically active phytochemicals to enhance 
      therapeutic efficacy and overcome treatment resistance. Capmatinib, a selective 
      MET inhibitor approved for MET-driven non-small cell lung cancer, and curcumin, a 
      natural polyphenol possessing antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and anticancer 
      activities, represent a promising therapeutic combination. This emerging paradigm 
      highlights the need for sensitive, sustainable, and efficient analytical 
      platforms capable of monitoring both compounds in pharmaceutical and biological 
      matrices. Accordingly, two new spectrofluorimetric methods were created and 
      validated, allowing for the quantification of capmatinib and the simultaneous 
      assay of capmatinib-curcumin combinations. The first method harnessed the unique 
      microenvironmental properties of sodium dodecyl sulfate as a monomeric denaturant 
      to markedly enhance the intrinsic fluorescence of capmatinib in an aqueous 
      medium, providing a linear response over 5.0-120.0 ng/mL with a detection limit 
      of 1.42 ng/mL. The second method exploited synchronous spectrofluorimetry 
      (Deltalambda = 160 nm) to resolve overlapping fluorescence signals, enabling 
      separation-free quantification of capmatinib and curcumin across concentration 
      ranges of 20.0-700.0 and 10.0-250.0 ng/mL, respectively. Both methods 
      demonstrated excellent selectivity, accuracy, and precision according to ICH 
      guidelines and were successfully applied to spiked human plasma samples.

TI  - Targeting the Tumor Microenvironment Network to Overcome Immunotherapy Resistance 
      in Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer: Mechanisms to Clinical Translation.
PG  - e72335
LID - 10.1002/cam4.72335 [doi]
LID - e72335
AB  - BACKGROUND: Immunotherapy has transformed the treatment landscape for advanced 
      non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), yet resistance mediated by the tumor 
      microenvironment (TME) limits its efficacy in most patients. This review aims to 
      synthesize the current understanding of the TME as an integrated network driving 
      immunotherapy resistance and to evaluate emerging therapeutic strategies aimed at 
      overcoming it. METHODS: We conducted a comprehensive narrative review of key 
      literature in the field, sourced from PubMed and major conference proceedings. 
      The focus was on mechanistic studies elucidating immunosuppressive cell circuits, 
      metabolic reprogramming, epigenetic regulation, spatial heterogeneity, and 
      microbiome interactions within the lung cancer TME. Preclinical and clinical 
      evidence for TME-targeted combination therapies and biomarker development was 
      also critically analyzed. RESULTS: The TME orchestrates resistance through highly 
      interconnected axes: (1) cellular circuits including myeloid-derived suppressor 
      cells (MDSCs), regulatory T cells (Tregs), and M2-polarized tumor-associated 
      macrophages (M2-TAMs), (2) metabolic barriers (lactate acidosis, nutrient 
      depletion), (3) epigenetic reprogramming, and (4) systemic communication via 
      exosomes and the microbiome. Spatial transcriptomics reveals that driver 
      mutations (e.g., EGFR, KRAS) shape distinct immune-excluded architectures. To 
      disrupt this network, rational combination strategies-such as immune checkpoint 
      inhibitors with metabolic modulators (LDHAi), epigenetic drugs, targeted agents 
      (METi), or microbiome interventions-show synergistic potential in early-phase 
      trials. Furthermore, a tiered biomarker system integrating spatial, metabolic, 
      and microbial features is proposed to guide patient stratification. CONCLUSION: 
      Overcoming immunotherapy resistance requires a paradigm shift from single-target 
      to network-targeted therapy. This review provides an integrated framework for 
      understanding TME-mediated resistance and translates these insights into a 
      roadmap for clinical translation. Future efforts must prioritize 
      biomarker-validated, rationally designed combination therapies to improve 
      outcomes for patients with resistant NSCLC.
CI  - (c) 2026 The Author(s). Cancer Medicine published by John Wiley & Sons Ltd.

TI  - First-line treatment outcomes in HER2-altered advanced NSCLC: a real-world 
      multi-center study comparing trastuzumab deruxtecan with conventional therapies 
      and analysis by HER2/EGFR co-alteration status.
LID - 10.1186/s12885-026-16688-7 [doi]
LID - 1220
AB  - BACKGROUND: First-line treatment options for HER2-altered advanced non-small cell 
      lung cancer (NSCLC) remain limited, especially in cases with HER2-EGFR 
      co-alterations. While new agents like trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd) have 
      emerged, its real-world evidence in first line remains limited. METHODS: This 
      study included patients with advanced NSCLC who had HER2 mutations or 
      amplification and received first-line systemic therapy at six medical centers. 
      Outcomes were evaluated following chemotherapy-based combinations, pan-HER 
      tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs), and T-DXd. Endpoints included progression-free 
      survival (PFS), objective response rate (ORR), disease control rate (DCR), 
      one-year and two-year survival rates, and toxicity. RESULTS: A total of 69 
      patients were enrolled, comprising 45 with only HER2 alterations and 24 with 
      HER2-EGFR co-alterations. In patients with only HER2-altered NSCLC, those 
      receiving first-line T-DXd had an mPFS of 15.7 months (95% CI, 12.5-18.9), an ORR 
      of 85.7%, and a DCR of 100.0%. The mPFS for patients receiving chemotherapy-based 
      regimens, pan-HER TKIs, and chemotherapy plus pan-HER TKIs were 7.6 (95% CI, 
      6.6-8.5) months, 4.4 (95% CI, 4.0-4.9) months, and 7.0 (95% CI, 6.1-9.1) months, 
      respectively. The ORR for the three groups above was 33.3%, 0, and 0, 
      respectively. In subgroup analysis, patients with only HER2 alterations who did 
      not harbor TP53 mutations had a longer PFS than those with TP53 co-mutations 
      (8.7 m vs. 7.2 m; HR: 0.35; 95% CI: 0.11-0.99; P = 0.048). In patients with 
      EGFR-HER2 co-alterations, first-line EGFR-TKI monotherapy yielded an mPFS of 9.7 
      (95% CI, 8.6-10.8) months, an ORR of 40.0%, and a DCR of 93.3%. Regarding safety, 
      we did not observe Grade >/= 3 TRAEs in first-line T-DXd treatment. CONCLUSIONS: 
      First-line T-DXd showed promising activity signals in only HER2-altered NSCLC. In 
      EGFR-HER2 co-altered patients, the EGFR-TKI monotherapy showed limited efficacy. 
      The findings underscored the urgent need for prospective randomised trials.
CI  - (c) 2026. The Author(s).

TI  - Benzisothiazole derivatives as KRAS G12C inhibitors for potential treatment of 
      non-small cell lung cancer.
PG  - 110601
LID - S0045-2068(26)01137-5 [pii]
LID - 10.1016/j.bioorg.2026.110601 [doi]
AB  - KRAS G12C mutation is a key driver of multiple solid tumors, and has recently 
      become a crucial target for therapy via small molecules that covalently bind to 
      the mutated cysteine. Herein, a series of benzisothiazole derivatives is 
      presented as KRAS G12C inhibitors utilizing a scaffold-hopping strategy. Among 
      them, S32 exhibited remarkable antiproliferative efficacy, specifically in the 
      NCI-H23 cell line (IC(50) = 0.55 +/- 0.05 muM). Docking studies of S32 revealed that 
      the oxygen atom of the trifluoroethoxy group at the C-7 position of 
      benzisothiazole formed a hydrogen bond with His95 and extended into a 
      solvent-exposed region. Additionally, S32 obviously inhibited nucleotide exchange 
      efficiency, stabilizing KRAS in the GDP-bound state and inhibited the downstream 
      ERK/AKT signaling pathways of KRAS in NCI-H23 and NCI-H358 cells, while inducing 
      apoptosis by upregulating BAX and downregulating BCL-2 apoptotic proteins. 
      Furthermore, S32 achieved a tumor growth inhibition (TGI) of 90.8% at a dose of 
      30 mg/kg. These results confirmed that S32, with a benzisothiazole scaffold, was 
      an effective KRAS G12C inhibitor, potentially promoting cancer therapy 
      development.

TI  - Effects of tyrosine kinase inhibitors in treatment of non small cell lung cancer 
      patients with brain metastases, real world experience.
LID - 10.1007/s11845-026-04653-z [doi]
AB  - BACKGROUND: Lung cancer remains one of the leading causes of cancer-related 
      mortality worldwide, and brain metastases are a common and serious complication 
      of advanced disease. Early detection and effective management of brain metastases 
      are essential for improving survival and maintaining quality of life. Recent 
      advances in targeted therapies, particularly tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs), 
      have shown promising efficacy in controlling both systemic and intracranial 
      disease. This study aimed to evaluate the efficacy of TKIs in patients with 
      non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) harboring driver mutations and presenting with 
      brain metastases. MATERIALS AND METHODS: We retrospectively analyzed 31 patients 
      with NSCLC and brain metastases who received targeted therapy at Karadeniz 
      Technical University Faculty of Medicine between January 1, 2010, and September 
      1, 2023. Clinical and demographic data, including age, sex, histological and 
      molecular subtypes, metastatic sites, progression-free survival (PFS), overall 
      survival (OS), and treatment modalities, were evaluated. RESULTS: Median OS was 
      34.5 months in patients with PD-L1 expression < 50%, compared with 4.5 months in 
      those with PD-L1 expression >/= 50% (p = 0.004). Median OS was 36.1 months (range, 
      1.8-58.7) among never-smokers and 4.5 months (range, 2.2-18.5) among current or 
      former smokers (p = 0.005). CONCLUSION: TKIs remain an effective treatment option 
      for patients with NSCLC and brain metastases. PD-L1 expression and smoking status 
      may influence clinical outcomes. Further studies are warranted to validate these 
      findings and clarify the prognostic role of PD-L1 expression in NSCLC patients 
      with brain metastases.

      Ireland.

TI  - Antibody-drug conjugates in lung cancer: clinical development, therapeutic 
      advances, and emerging challenges.
LID - 10.1007/s00280-026-04964-z [doi]
LID - 118
AB  - Antibody-drug conjugates (ADCs) are designed to deliver cytotoxic agents directly 
      to tumor cells by conjugating an antigen-specific monoclonal antibody with a 
      potent payload. By recognizing tumor-associated antigens and undergoing 
      intracellular processing, ADCs enhance antitumor activity while minimizing damage 
      to normal tissues. This review focuses on the research progress of ADCs targeting 
      non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) and small cell lung cancer (SCLC), providing 
      an in-depth analysis of their mechanisms of action, molecular design, and 
      clinical efficacy, with particular emphasis on novel ADCs targeting HER2, TROP2, 
      and DLL3. Despite challenges such as target specificity and drug resistance, 
      clinical trial data demonstrate that ADCs exhibit significant therapeutic 
      efficacy in the treatment of both NSCLC and SCLC. The article also discusses 
      emerging strategies to overcome current limitations, including innovative linker 
      technologies, development of bispecific ADCs, and exploration of combination 
      treatment plans. Given that lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related 
      mortality worldwide, ADCs technology holds promise for delivering breakthrough 
      advances in improving therapeutic outcomes and alleviating the societal burden of 
      the disease. With ongoing research into ADCs, future clinical applications may 
      provide more precise and effective treatment options for patients with lung 
      cancer.
CI  - (c) 2026. The Author(s).

TI  - Systematic review of spectral computed tomography quantitative parameters for 
      predicting EGFR mutation status in non-small cell lung cancer.
PG  - 825
LID - 10.21037/qims-2025-1-2542 [doi]
LID - 825
AB  - BACKGROUND: Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) gene mutations are important 
      therapeutic targets in non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC). Spectral computed 
      tomography (spectral CT) has shown promise in the non-invasive prediction of EGFR 
      status; however, the diagnostic value of these parameters remains unclear. This 
      systematic review aimed to evaluate the utility of key quantitative spectral CT 
      parameters for predicting EGFR mutation status in NSCLC. METHODS: A comprehensive 
      search of several Chinese and English databases was conducted to retrieve 
      relevant diagnostic studies published up to October 1, 2025 that used pathology 
      as the gold standard to assess the predictive value of spectral CT quantitative 
      parameters for EGFR mutation detection in NSCLC. Two researchers conducted the 
      literature screening and data extraction independently. Due to study 
      heterogeneity, results were synthesized narratively. Risk of bias was assessed 
      using the Quality Assessment of Diagnostic Accuracy Studies 2 (QUADAS-2) tool. 
      RESULTS: The quantitative parameters of spectral CT, including iodine 
      concentration (IC), normalized iodine concentration (NIC), slope of the spectral 
      Hounsfield unit curve (lambdaHU), and effective atomic number (Zeff), were higher in 
      the EGFR-mutant cohort than in the wild-type cohort (P<0.05). Multi-parameter 
      models combining quantitative parameters with clinical and imaging features 
      showed improved diagnostic performance. The results were unaffected by the 
      various brands of CT scanners and their scanning parameters. CONCLUSIONS: Several 
      quantitative parameters derived from spectral CT are associated with EGFR 
      mutations in NSCLC. Predictive models integrating these quantitative parameters 
      with imaging features and clinical variables may further improve diagnostic 
      efficacy. This may inform epidermal growth factor receptor tyrosine kinase 
      inhibitor (EGFR-TKI) planing. Large-scale prospective studies are needed to 
      validate these models.
CI  - (c) AME Publishing Company.

TI  - A study to learn how well HER3-DXd (patritumab deruxtecan) works and how safe it 
      is in people with previously treated non-small-cell lung cancer that has a 
      mutation in the EGFR gene: a plain language summary of the HERTHENA-Lung01 study.
PG  - 1-16
LID - 10.1080/14796694.2026.2691681 [doi]
FAU - Yu, Helena A
AU  - Yu HA
AD  - aDepartment of Medicine, Medical Oncology, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer 
      Center, New York, United States.
FAU - Goto, Yasushi
AU  - Goto Y

FAU - Janne, Pasi A
AU  - Janne PA
AD  - cDepartment of Medical Oncology, Dana-Farber Cancer Institute Department of

AD  - dHarvard Medical School, Massachusetts, Boston, United States.
AD  - eDepartment of Medicine, Brigham and Women's Hospital, Massachusetts, Boston, 
      United States.
LA  - eng
SI  - ClinicalTrials.gov/NCT04619004
PT  - Journal Article
DEP - 20261012
PL  - England
TA  - Future Oncol
JT  - Future oncology (London, England)
JID - 101256629
SB  - IM
OAB - What is this summary about?This is a summary of the publication about the 
      HERTHENA-Lung01 study, which was published in the Journal of Clinical Oncology in 
      September 2023. The study included 277 adults with non-small-cell lung cancer 
      (NSCLC) that had a mutation in a gene called EGFR. The researchers wanted to know 
      whether a study drug called HER3-DXd (patritumab deruxtecan) could improve 
      participants' cancer after it had grown after previous treatment with certain 
      other cancer drugs.What are the key takeaways?About 3 out of every 10 
      participants responded to treatment with HER3-DXd at some point during the study, 
      which means their cancer shrank significantly or disappeared. Among the 
      participants who benefited, about half had their cancer continue to respond to 
      HER3-DXd for longer than 6.4 months before growing or spreading.After starting 
      treatment with HER3-DXd, about half of the participants lived for longer than 
      about 5.5 months before their cancer grew, spread, or caused death, and for 
      longer than about 11.9 months overall.For participants with brain metastases, 1 
      out of every 3 participants had their brain metastases respond to treatment with 
      HER3-DXd. About half of these participants had their brain metastases continue to 
      respond to HER3-DXd for longer than about 8 months.About 6 out of every 10 
      participants had a severe new medical issue during the study, either related or 
      unrelated to HER3-DXd.Almost all participants had a new medical issue during the 
      study that was possibly related to HER3-DXd. About 1 out of every 20 participants 
      had interstitial lung disease (ILD) or pneumonitis that was possibly related to 
      HER3-DXd.What were the main conclusions reported by the researchers?HER3-DXd has 
      been shown to be effective for people who have NSCLC with certain EGFR mutations 
      and whose cancer has progressed after multiple previous treatments. People who 
      receive HER3-DXd should be watched closely for signs of interstitial lung disease 
      (ILD) or pneumonitis. These are the results from one study, and other studies 
      might provide different results. This study did not directly compare HER3-DXd 
      with any other cancer treatments. Always talk to your doctor before making any 
      changes to your medication. Further studies with HER3-DXd are planned and 
      multiple studies are ongoing.Clinical trial number: NCT04619004.

TI  - How zongertinib was developed for HER2-mutant non-small cell lung cancer: a plain 
      language summary.
PG  - 1-8
LID - 10.1080/14737140.2026.2731196 [doi]
FAU - Heymach, John V
AU  - Heymach JV
AD  - aDepartment of Thoracic/Head and Neck Medical Oncology, University of Texas MD 
      Anderson Cancer Center, Houston, TX, USA.
FAU - Wilding, Birgit
AU  - Wilding B
AD  - bDepartment of Medicinal Chemistry, Boehringer Ingelheim RCV GmbH & Co. KG, 
      Vienna, Austria.
LA  - eng
PT  - Journal Article
DEP - 20261011
PL  - England
TA  - Expert Rev Anticancer Ther
JT  - Expert review of anticancer therapy
JID - 101123358
SB  - IM
OAB - What is this summary about?Non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is the most common 
      type of lung cancer. About 2-4% of people with NSCLC have a type of DNA change 
      known as a mutation in a gene called HER2 (human epidermal growth factor receptor 
      2). This type of lung cancer is called HER2-mutant NSCLC.Until recently, 
      trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd) was the only approved medicine that targets HER2 
      in people with HER2- mutant NSCLC. However, T-DXd must be given through an 
      intravenous drip (directly into a vein) at a hospital or clinic. It can also 
      cause side effects that can be difficult to manage. There was therefore a need 
      for other HER2-targeted treatments that are more convenient, while minimizing 
      side effects.Zongertinib was designed to selectively block the activity of HER2, 
      with the aim of reducing side effects compared with some other medicines. As it 
      is now available for doctors to use in the clinic, this summary explains how 
      zongertinib works, what researchers found in clinical studies, and how it may 
      provide a new treatment option for people living with HER2-mutant NSCLC.What are 
      the key takeaways?Zongertinib is an oral medicine (taken as a tablet) called a 
      tyrosine kinase inhibitor that specifically targets HER2. It was developed to 
      address the unmet need for a treatment for HER2-mutant NSCLC that could 
      specifically block HER2.In the Beamion LUNG-1 trial, zongertinib helped shrink 
      tumors or stop them from growing in people who had already received treatment for 
      HER2- mutant NSCLC. Most side effects experienced by patients were mild. 
      Zongertinib provides a new targeted treatment option for people with HER2-mutant 
      NSCLC.

PMID- 42860723
OWN - NLM

DP  - 2026 Oct 11
TI  - Spatial Atlas of Small Cell Lung Carcinoma Reveals Intratumoral Tumor-State 
      Heterogeneity and an MMP12(+) Macrophage Niche.
LID - 10.1111/cas.70555 [doi]
AB  - Small-cell lung carcinoma (SCLC) is a highly aggressive tumor with a dismal 
      prognosis among lung cancers. Treatment is failing because of primary or rapidly 
      developing secondary resistance. Recent molecular studies have suggested four 
      SCLC subtypes with unique therapeutic vulnerabilities-ASCL1, NEUROD1, POU2F3, and 
      YAP. These subtypes still distinguish themselves by considerable intra-tumoral 
      heterogeneity and the influence of the microenvironmental context, which are both 
      considered to be responsible for treatment resistance. We integrated in situ 
      hybridization-based spatial transcriptomics with histological and 
      protein-expression analyses of 18 specimens, comprising 16 SCLCs, one atypical 
      carcinoid, and one normal lung specimen. Systematic case-by-case analysis 
      revealed multiple coexisting tumor states, including neuroendocrine and 
      non-neuroendocrine components, regional hypoxic states, and case-specific 
      FABP7-positive and loss-of-chromosome-Y-associated populations. Integrative 
      transcriptome clustering, copy number estimation, and exploratory trajectory 
      analysis revealed transcriptional relationships among spatially coexisting tumor 
      states, including phenotypically distinct tumor components with molecular 
      features consistent with close clonal relationships in selected heterogeneous 
      tumors. Furthermore, we identified immune signatures and uncovered a previously 
      unrecognized MMP12-positive tumor-associated macrophage population, enriched at 
      the tumor interface and spatially associated with necrotic and hypoxic regions. 
      In vitro co-culture experiments further showed that direct contact with SCLC 
      cells increased MMP12 expression in macrophages, with hypoxia further enhancing 
      its expression under specific conditions. Together, our study delineates the 
      complex spatial architecture of SCLC by defining specific cellular programs and 
      adjacent immune phenotypes that provide an organizational framework for 
      understanding its marked intratumoral heterogeneity.

      Ltd on behalf of Japanese Cancer Association.

DP  - 2026 Oct
TI  - Metastatic Lung Adenocarcinoma to the Uterus Mimicking a Primary Uterine 
      Malignancy: A Case Report.
PG  - e70706
LID - 10.1002/cnr2.70706 [doi]
LID - e70706
AB  - BACKGROUND: Lung adenocarcinoma is the most prevalent subtype of non-small cell 
      lung cancer and exhibits a high tendency for distant metastasis. However, uterine 
      metastasis with synchronous multiorgan involvement is extremely rare and prone to 
      misdiagnosis due to atypical clinical presentations. To date, fewer than 20 cases 
      of lung adenocarcinoma metastasizing to the uterus have been documented globally, 
      most of which present with typical gynecological symptoms, making our atypical 
      case highly educational and complementary to existing clinical data. CASE: 
      Herein, we describe a 77-year-old postmenopausal female patient who initially 
      presented with lumbago and dizziness without typical pulmonary or gynecological 
      symptoms, with an ECOG performance status of 1. Imaging studies raised suspicion 
      of a primary uterine malignancy, but pathological biopsy, immunohistochemistry, 
      and genomic testing ultimately confirmed advanced lung adenocarcinoma with 
      multiorgan metastases (uterus, lung, liver, bone). Molecular analysis identified 
      an EGFR exon 21 p.L858R mutation, and the patient received palliative local 
      radiotherapy combined with osimertinib targeted therapy. During treatment, the 
      patient developed mild radiation-induced fatigue and slight myelosuppression, 
      which were fully relieved after symptomatic supportive care, with no severe 
      unexpected adverse events observed. CONCLUSION: This case illustrates the 
      atypical metastatic pattern of lung adenocarcinoma, underscores the pivotal role 
      of pathological and immunohistochemical examinations in differentiating primary 
      from metastatic tumors, and offers a reference for managing diagnostically 
      challenging cases. Early and accurate diagnosis coupled with genomic-guided 
      personalized therapy is critical for enhancing quality of life and prognosis in 
      patients with advanced lung cancer.

PMID- 42860233

DP  - 2026 Oct 11
TI  - Blockade of mutant RAS oncogenic signaling: A 2026 update.
PG  - 109669
LID - S1043-6618(26)01584-7 [pii]
LID - 10.1016/j.phrs.2026.109669 [doi]
AB  - RAS proteins (HRAS, KRAS, NRAS) contribute to many physiological signal 
      transduction pathways involving cell growth, cell division, and survival. RAS 
      proteins possess GTPase activity. These proteins toggle between inactive and 
      active forms; the conversion of inactive RAS-GDP to active RAS-GTP is facilitated 
      by guanine nucleotide exchange factors (GEFs) turning the switch on and the 
      intrinsic RAS-GTPase activity accelerated by the GTPase activating proteins 
      (GAPs) turning the switch off. RAS is the upstream component of the 
      RAS-RAF-MEK-ERK and the PI3-kinase-AKT signaling cascades. One-fifth of all 
      cancers bear RAS mutations and about 75% of them involve KRAS. Except for the 
      GTP/GDP binding site, RAS proteins lack deep surface pockets able to bind 
      high-affinity inhibitors, which impeded the development of specific antagonists. 
      KRAS mutations occur frequently in pancreatic, lung, and colorectal cancers, 
      which are among the deadliest cancers worldwide. RAS mutations include those of 
      codon 12 G12D/V/C/R, codon 13 G13D, and codon 61 Q61R/K/L. Studies conducted over 
      the last 15 years led to the discovery of the so-called RAS switch II pocket. 
      Sotorasib and adagrasib, which interact with this pocket and directly block the 
      action of KRAS following the covalent modification of KRAS C12, are FDA-approved 
      for the second-line treatment of KRAS(G12C)-mutant non-small cell lung cancer. 
      Daraxonrasib, which is an indirect inhibitor of wildtype and mutant H/K/NRAS 
      isoforms, is FDA approved for the second-line treatment of all RAS-mutant 
      pancreatic cancers. Daraxonrasib first forms a complex with cyclophilin A (a 
      chaperone protein), which then interacts with active RAS to block RAS signaling.
